### 1. Name the Fields

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Map the Stones
- Title: Name the Fields
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: After the Nile flood, the boundaries were resurveyed because the water had moved them. The stone was replaced not according to memory but according to the original measurement. Map the four fields from the same starting question: what is actually mine?
- Words: Do not move the markers on the boundaries of the fields, nor alter the measuring line.
- Steps:
  1. Map four domains: resources, labor, credit, and force.
  2. For each, write what is actually yours and what belongs to others.
  3. For force, name what level of pressure or authority is proportionate to the situation.
- Source note: The boundary-stone surveyors worked from original records, not from what was convenient after the flood. This sitting maps four fields — resources, labor, credit, force — from the same starting question: what is actually mine, and where does someone else begin?
- Requires real-world action: false

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Do not move the markers on the boundaries of the fields, nor alter the measuring line."

Steps
1. Map four domains: resources, labor, credit, and force.
2. For each, write what is actually yours and what belongs to others.
3. For force, name what level of pressure or authority is proportionate to the situation.
```

  2. Mark any domain where the measure is genuinely unclear.
  3. Name where you may be taking from not knowing the line rather than from actual entitlement.

- Event number: 3

### 4. The Honest Survey

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Assess the Survey
- Title: The Honest Survey
- Timing: Day 11 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The resurvey asked one question per field: where does the stone actually belong? Not where it ended up after the flood — where the original measurement said it should be.
- Words: One who transgresses the furrow shortens a lifetime.
- Steps:
  1. For resources, ask whether you are consuming at the level that is actually yours.
  2. For labor, ask whether you are doing your work, someone else's work, or leaving yours to others.
  3. For credit, ask whether you are claiming what was shared or someone else's.
  4. For force, ask whether pressure or authority has continued past what the situation required.
- Source note: The re-survey after the Nile flood was restorative, not creative — it found where the stone belonged before disruption moved it. Not where the boundary has drifted, but where it should be if the original measure were applied honestly.
- Requires real-world action: false

- Event number: 5

- Title: The Gullet's Evidence
- Timing: Day 15 · any time · 11:00 reminder
- Duration: 5-5 min
- Purpose: What was taken past the measure is already being returned through a different mechanism. The gullet's rejection is not a metaphor — it is the actual consequence operating in the relationship, the situation, or the body of the one who took too much.
- Words: The property of a dependent is an obstruction to the throat. Too much bread is swallowed and spat up.
- Steps:
  1. Where the stone moved, name what life is already throwing back: strain, resentment, depletion, or loss of trust.
  2. Name the evidence without defending it.
  3. Write what enough would have looked like in that domain.
- Source note: Amenemope's image of the gullet rejecting what was taken past measure is viscerally physical — bread swallowed and spat up, property that becomes an obstruction in the throat. The excess does not stay. It produces visible evidence that the measure was exceeded.
- Requires real-world action: false

- Words: What is mine I hold. What is not mine I have set down. The fields are measured. The stones are placed.

## 20. Hotep

- Key: hotep
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: What you have given is enough. Cool your heart.
- Overview: A 30-day evening practice for the peace of completed offering: name what was given, distinguish real obligation from fear of tomorrow, and let the heart cool before sleep.
- Confidence: Draws on htp offering language, cooling passages, and the peace of completed presentation.

- Glyph: 𓊵
- Routing: Best for inability to stop, compulsive overwork, fear of tomorrow, and the feeling that the offering is never enough.
- Notes prefix: hotep
- Behavior kind: maat_hotep_event
- Burden: Low
- Rhythm: Decan
- Graph node slugs:
  1. maat
  2. hapy
  3. anpu
  4. heru
### 1. Name the Offering

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Name the Offering
- Title: Name the Offering
- Timing: Day 1 · evening
- Duration: 3-5 min
- Purpose: The offering is complete when it meets the measure of what was owed — not when the giver has given everything possible. This distinction is the practice.
- Words: I have come having gotten Horus's eye, that your heart may become cool with it.
- Steps:
  1. Place water on your surface and sit somewhere you actually rest. Not a working position — a resting one. Hotep does not happen at the desk.
  2. Write what you are currently offering: time, labor, care, attention, skill.
  3. Write what is owed. The offering is complete when it meets the measure, not when you can give no more.
  4. Drink the water.
- Source note: The Pyramid Texts begin restoration with cool water because the heart that has been cooled can receive the offering's completion. The cool heart is what Hotep produces — not what you must bring to it.
- Requires real-world action: false

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words

Steps

2. Write what you are currently offering: time, labor, care, attention, skill.
3. Write what is owed. The offering is complete when it meets the measure, not when you can give no more.
4. Drink the water.
```

  2. Ask whether there is a real gap between what was owed and what has been given.
  3. Write one sentence about the gap, or its absence.

```text
2. Ask whether it is real incompleteness or the distortion that more is always owed.
3. Carry that distinction into Decan 2.
```

### 4. The Question: Is It Done?

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Is It Complete?
- Title: The Question: Is It Done?
- Timing: Day 11 · evening
- Duration: 3-5 min
- Purpose: The central question of Hotep is not "have I done enough" — it is "have I done what was owed." These produce different answers.
- Words: Speak this before the question: Do not go to bed fearing tomorrow. God is success; man is failure. Then sit with the question for at least two minutes: Has the offering of this period been made?
- Steps:
  1. Sit with the question for at least two minutes: Has the offering of this period been made?
  2. Answer with the Day 1 measure, not with a vague feeling of enough.
  3. Write: The offering is complete, or The offering is incomplete in [specific way].
- Source note: Amenemope does not address fear of tomorrow with productivity advice. The answer is distinguishing between what is yours to do and what is tomorrow's problem. The offering made today closes today's account.
- Requires real-world action: false

### 9. The Cool Heart

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Receive the Hotep
- Title: The Cool Heart
- Timing: Day 29 · evening
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The cool heart is the product of the completed offering, not its prerequisite. This sitting produces the cool heart by closing the offering record and placing what cannot be controlled outside the sleeping space.
- Words: These your cool waters have come from your son. Your heart will not become weary with it. Hotep.
- Steps:
  1. Place water and sit where you will be before sleep.
  2. Name aloud what you offered across this flow: time, labor, care, presence, skill.
  3. Speak: What was owed has been given.
  4. Write anything left in the bed that is not yours to control and place the paper away from where you sleep. The act is physical because the cool heart is physical.
  5. Drink the water. Lie down.
  6. sleep
- Source note: The ḥtp formula began with "Hotep" — satisfaction received, offering made — and the provision followed. The peace comes first; the provision follows from it. This final event replicates that sequence: the record is closed, the water is drunk, the heart is cooled.

## 21. The Open Mouth

- Key: the-open-mouth
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: What comes out of your mouth is what you are creating.
- Overview: A 30-day speech practice: record what your mouth has been creating, govern the tongue through one discipline, and say one important thing that has been withheld.
- Confidence: Draws on the formative power of speech in the Memphite Theology and Opening of the Mouth ritual.

- Glyph: 𓂋
- Routing: Best for heated speech, hasty response, slander, careless words, and important truth left unsaid.
- Notes prefix: open_mouth
- Behavior kind: maat_open_mouth_event
- Burden: Low
- Rhythm: Decan
- Graph node slugs:
  1. maat
  2. ptah
  3. djehuty
  4. aset
### 1. Open the Mouth's Record

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: The Inventory
- Title: Open the Mouth's Record
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: What comes out of the mouth before the heart has governed it is not yet speech — it is reflex with sound. This sitting finds the reflex patterns before they are taken for speech.
- Words: It is the heart and the tongue that have power. I open the record of what my tongue has been commanding.
- Steps:
  1. Write what your mouth produces when you are tired, frustrated, or thoughtless.
  2. Name what you are saying that should not be said.
  3. Name what is inaccurate or overheated.
  4. Name what needs to be said but has been swallowed.
- Source note: In the Memphite Theology, the heart conceives and the tongue commands — formation happens through speech. The mouth that produces without the heart's governance is not creating order; it is creating noise. This sitting opens the record of which category most of the recent speech has fallen into.
- Requires real-world action: false

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"It is the heart and the tongue that have power. I open the record of what my tongue has been commanding."

Steps
1. Write what your mouth produces when you are tired, frustrated, or thoughtless.
2. Name what you are saying that should not be said.
3. Name what is inaccurate or overheated.
4. Name what needs to be said but has been swallowed.
```

  2. Choose one thing not said and write what its absence created.
  3. Name the speech pattern from Day 1 that Decan 2 should govern.

- Event number: 3

- Title: Seal the Mouth's Record

- Purpose: Choose one speech discipline and one important thing that needs to be said.
- Words: Repeat only what is seen, not what is heard. Do not repeat slander.

### 4. The Governed Mouth Opens

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: The Governed Mouth
- Title: The Governed Mouth Opens
- Timing: Day 11 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The tongue is the steering oar, not the pilot. The heart holds course; the tongue carries it out. This decan names the one discipline that keeps the oar from steering where the heart didn't direct it.
- Words: Be strong in your heart. Do not steer with your tongue.
- Steps:
  1. Review the five disciplines: pause, accuracy, relevance, timing, witness before speaking.
  2. For each, write whether it is currently operating in your speech.
  3. Choose one discipline for this decan and define the specific practice.
- Source note: Amenemope's image of the tongue as a steering oar places the problem clearly: an oar not controlled by the navigator creates the illusion of movement while taking the vessel in the wrong direction. Choose the discipline most absent.
- Requires real-world action: false

- Words: Do not pour out your words to others. Be strong in your heart.
- Steps:

  2. Check the thing that needs to be said: has it been said? If not, when before the decan ends?

- Timing: Day 21 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The heart conceives before the tongue declares. Writing the true thing first is the heart's work — making it real through speech is the tongue's work. This sitting completes the first half.
- Words: Your tongue is the plummet. Your heart is the weight. I open my mouth with care.
- Steps:
  1. Ask what your mouth has been creating across this flow.
  2. Name one specific, true, currently unspoken thing you will bring into the world by saying it.
  3. Write it first. What is not fully conceived is not ready to be declared.
- Source note: The Memphite Theology describes creation as: heart conceives, tongue declares, world forms. The mouth that speaks before the heart has finished conceiving is interrupting the process. Writing the sentence first is the heart's work completed before the tongue takes over.

### 9. The Open Mouth

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Mouth That Creates
- Title: The Open Mouth
- Timing: Day 29 · evening
- Duration: 8-10 min
- Purpose: The flow closes with what was spoken that needed to be spoken, and with one minute of intentional silence. The silence is not the absence of speech — it is the space the governed mouth creates.
- Words: Speak only what is true: My mouth is open. My speech is governed. What I command, I create with care. Then sit in intentional silence for one full minute.
- Steps:
  1. Name the most significant speech pattern the inventory revealed.
  2. Name what the governance practice produced.
  3. Name what was spoken that needed to be spoken: I said [thing] on [day]. It is now in the world.
  4. Speak only the true lines: my speech was not heated; my heart was not hasty; I said what needed to be said.
  5. Sit in intentional silence for one full minute.
- Source note: The Opening of the Mouth ceremony restored speech, eating, and breath after they had been closed. This close makes silence available — the governed mouth can be silent when silence is right. The one minute at the end is the practice of that.

## 22. The Living Record

- Key: the-living-record
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: What is recorded persists. Build the record of this decan.
- Overview: A 30-day practice of building a genuine decan account across ḥꜣw: day card, node library, planner, journal, feed, alignment, Flow Studio, guidance, and a physical record.
- Confidence: Draws on Kemetic logbooks, royal annals, autobiographies, and scribal record practice.

- Glyph: 𓏞
- Routing: Best for positive ḥꜣw practice, new-user feature integration, decan record keeping, journaling, guidance engagement, and users ready to use the full app suite intentionally.
- Notes prefix: living_record
- Behavior kind: maat_living_record_event
- Burden: Low

### 1. Open the Day Card

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Enter the Record
- Title: Open the Day Card
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 5-5 min
- Purpose: The day card is the record's first entry — the dating before the work. Merer's logbook placed the date before naming the work because the work outside of time is not a record.
- Words: What occurred must be placed within its time. What is placed within its time becomes record. What becomes record persists.
- Steps:
  1. Open today's day card in ḥꜣw and read the Kemetic date, decan name, Ma'at principle, and cosmic context.
  2. Write the date down outside the app: Kemetic date, season, and decan name.
  3. Speak the line. The record has been opened.
- Source note: Merer's logbook dated each entry before naming the work because work outside of time is not a record — it is an anecdote. The day card does the same: it places the decan's activity inside the Kemetic count before anything is added to it.
- Requires real-world action: true

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Write the date down outside the app: Kemetic date, season, and decan name.
```

- Source note: The House of Life was not a passive library. It preserved sacred texts so they could be read, copied, interpreted, and used.

- Event number: 4

- Section: Build the Record
- Title: Write in the Journal
- Timing: Day 11 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The journal is the papyrus — the private record from which the guidance system builds its account. What is written with honesty and specificity produces more accurate guidance than what is performed there with care for appearance.
- Words: Their writing was their memory-priest. The pen was their child. Their names endured.
- Steps:
  1. Open the journal and write at least three substantive sentences.
  2. Write at least three substantive sentences about what this decan has meant, what you actually did in Ma'at, what resisted the period, and one unanswered question. Specific is more useful than comprehensive.
  3. Notice any badges generated from the entry. They are signals that the record is being read.
- Source note: Chester Beatty IV calls writing the memory-priest that keeps the name alive. The journal's entries are that priest — they build the account the guidance system works from. Honest and specific entries produce honest and specific guidance.
- Requires real-world action: true

## 23. Het-Heru

- Key: het-heru
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: The destroying eye and the goddess of joy are the same force.
- Overview: A 30-day practice of transforming the hot Sekhmet force into Het-Heru: name what has gone too far, find the red beer that fills the need, then practice music, feast, beauty, and joy.
- Confidence: Draws on Sekhmet-Hathor transformation, the Feast of Drunkenness, and Hathor as Mistress of Joy.

- Glyph: 𓉡
- Routing: Best for joy, beauty, music, delight, creative heat, rage that has become self-perpetuating, and users who need transformation through abundance rather than suppression.
- Notes prefix: het_heru
- Behavior kind: maat_het_heru_event
- Burden: Low
- Rhythm: Decan
- Graph node slugs:
  1. maat
  2. hathor
  3. sekhmet
  4. eye_of_ra
  5. ra
### 1. Find Your Sekhmet

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: The Eye Goes Out
- Title: Find Your Sekhmet
- Timing: Day 1 · any time · 11:00 reminder
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: Sekhmet was sent by Ra for a legitimate reason, against a real threat. The force going further than intended is not a character flaw — it is what happens when the Eye has no beer. Name yours without assigning fault to its origin.
- Words: Sekhmet, the Powerful One, the Eye of Ra, went out and did not come back when called. She is the same force as Het-Heru before the beer.
- Steps:
  1. Read the core story: Ra sent the Eye as Sekhmet, she kept destroying after he called her back, and the gods flooded the field with red beer.
  2. Name your Sekhmet: resentment, ambition, grief, perfectionism, anger, or another force with its own momentum.
  3. Write: My Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It was sent out for [wound or purpose]. It is still going because [what sustains it].
  4. Optional: name the beautiful thing this force used to make before it went too far.
- Source note: In the Book of the Heavenly Cow, Sekhmet's origin is Ra's wound and his legitimate anger. The force was right at the beginning. The problem arrived when it could not be recalled. This sitting names the force and its original purpose before deciding what the beer should be.
- Requires real-world action: false

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

"Sekhmet, the Powerful One, the Eye of Ra, went out and did not come back when called. She is the same force as Het-Heru before the beer."

3. Write: My Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It was sent out for [wound or purpose]. It is still going because [what sustains it].
4. Optional: name the beautiful thing this force used to make before it went too far.
```

- Title: The Scale of the Slaughter
- Timing: Day 5 · any time · 11:00 reminder

- Purpose: Assess whether the force is still serving its original purpose or has developed destructive momentum.
- Words: She was wading through blood. She had gone beyond the point where a command could reach her.
- Steps:

  2. Name who or what has entered its path that you did not intend to harm.
  3. Write without shame: the force may have been appropriate at the beginning; the question is whether it is still under direction.
- Requires real-world action: false

- Words: Ra looked at the field and did not meet Sekhmet with more force. He asked what would fill her.

  2. Write: My Sekhmet is looking for [the underlying need].

### 4. Find the Beer

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: The Red Beer
- Title: Find the Beer
- Timing: Day 11 · any time · 11:00 reminder
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The beer must be abundant. Ra did not pour one jar — he poured seven thousand. Whatever the transforming thing is, it must be given generously enough that the underlying need is actually met.
- Words: Seven thousand jars of beer were dyed red like blood. The force received what it could drink, and it was transformed.
- Steps:
  1. Name your beer: beauty, music, rest, connection, significance, touch, play, or another abundant good.
  2. Write: The beer for my Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It fills the actual need because [reason].
  3. Make it abundant. Ra did not pour one jar; he flooded the field.
- Source note: The myth names the scale: seven thousand jars, poured across the whole field of Lower Egypt. Transformation through a token gesture is not the Kemetic model. The beer that transforms must be given in the quantity that allows the need to be fully met.
- Requires real-world action: false

## 24. The Shore

- Key: the-shore
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: What is in your boat? Bring what you actually have.
- Overview: A 30-day practice of honest exchange: inventory what you can offer, prepare one real exchange, make it at honest measure, and account for what returns.
- Confidence: The Shore draws on attested exchange practice, deben-weighted value, Amenemope balance language, and The Shipwrecked Sailor. This household flow reconstructs honest exchange at modern scale.

- Glyph: 𓈘
- Routing: Best for money, exchange, value, offers, sales, negotiation, resources, business, clients, and honest material return.
- Notes prefix: shore

### 1. What Is in Your Boat?

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: What Is in Your Boat?
- Title: What Is in Your Boat?
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 8-8 min
- Purpose: The sailor offered oil to the Prince of Punt — the main product of the island he was standing on. The Prince laughed. Honest inventory prevents that error.
- Words: Do well, and you will attain influence.
- Steps:
  1. Write an honest inventory of skills, labor, knowledge, and relationships you can offer now.
  2. Mark each item as active, cultivated but unused, or undeveloped.
  3. Finish: My boat is primarily full of ___.
- Source note: The Shipwrecked Sailor arrived with nothing and offered what the island already had. The exchange that worked was not the commercial offer — it was his honest presence and his true account of the situation. What is actually in your boat, not what you wish were there?
- Requires real-world action: false

- Event number: 8

- Timing: Day 25 · any time · 11:00 reminder

- Purpose: Amenemope returns to this formulation three times — not as a preference for simplicity over wealth, but as a diagnostic. Anxious bread means something in the exchange was not at honest measure.
- Words: Better is bread when the mind is at ease than riches with anxiety.
- Steps:
  1. Eat something while doing this event. The bread test is practical: can this bread be eaten without planning how to protect what you have, or how to get more before it runs out?
  2. Ask whether this gain feels easy or anxious.
  3. Name what made it peaceful, or what made it uneasy.
- Source note: Amenemope's formulation is a diagnostic, not a moral preference. Bread eaten with an easy mind means the exchange was at honest measure. Bread eaten with anxiety means something went wrong in the gaining or the holding.
- Requires real-world action: false

  2. Name the capacities developed through years of work, failure, repetition, and attention.
  3. Write what you can actually do now, without inflation or deflation.
- Source note: The Kemetic autobiography opened with offices held and capacities demonstrated — not as a résumé but as a fact-check on what the years had actually produced. The capacities named here are the ones that required the full span to develop.

## 26. The True Name

- Key: the-true-name

- Overview: A 30-day private self-accounting flow: identify a false account, measure it against the real record, speak the accurate account aloud, and act from it.
- Confidence: The True Name draws on Ren theology, Aset’s name-work, and declaration before the scale. This household flow frames identity as accurate account, not diagnosis.

- Glyph: 𓂋𓈖
- Routing: Best for identity, confidence, self doubt, false belief, shame, accurate account, and acting from truth through self-accounting.

### 1. What Account Are You Operating From?

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: False Account
- Title: What Account Are You Operating From?
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 8-10 min
- Purpose: The account that feels like "just the truth about me" is the one most worth examining. The account you chose to believe has friction; the one that feels like fact has been operating as premise long enough to lose its edges.
- Words: The first account is not your fault; it is the account to be measured.
- Steps:
  1. Complete: People like me generally ___. When it comes to ___, I am someone who ___. The thing I quietly believe about myself is ___.
  2. Do not judge it yet and do not share this material.
  3. If what surfaces feels larger than this flow can hold, pause the flow and seek support.
- Source note: The Ren — the name — was a constituent part of the person, as real as the body. A name given by someone else and accepted as your own still operates as your name. This sitting finds the name you have been living under and asks whether it belongs to you.
- Requires real-world action: false

```text
Words
```

- Words: The Library lives when it is read.
- Steps:

  3. Write one new thought the entry opened in you.
- Source note: The Kemetic scribe copied what was assigned and what was sought out. The entry that arrests your attention is the one the Per Ankh is directing you to. Read it the way a scribe reads to copy: without assuming you know how it ends.

## 28. The Clearing

- Key: the-clearing
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: Its fruit is something sweet, its shade is pleasant, and it reaches its end in a grove.
- Overview: A 30-day practice of temperance before response: identify where heat drives action, create space before reply, and act once from the cleared state.
- Confidence: The Clearing draws on Instruction of Amenemope’s teaching of the temperate person as a tree in a sunlit field.

### 1. Find the Indoor Tree

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Indoor Tree
- Title: Find the Indoor Tree
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 8-8 min
- Purpose: The indoor tree is growing toward the available light, not toward the actual light. The direction it has been growing is visible in what it has been producing — and in what the heat has been costing.
- Words: The hot-headed man is like a tree grown in an enclosed space.
- Steps:
  1. Name one situation where heat or reaction drives your actions before the cleared state is available.
  2. Write the specific situation and what generates the heat.
  3. Record what the heat-driven pattern has cost.
- Source note: Amenemope's image is precise: the indoor tree loses its foliage in a moment because it grew toward whatever light was available, not toward the actual sun. The heat-driven response does the same — it goes toward the available outlet, not the right one.
- Requires real-world action: false

- Timing: Day 11 · morning

  1. Choose one physical or procedural act that creates space before response. Specific enough to follow without deciding in the moment. "Try to be calmer" is not the act. "Before responding to [situation], I will [specific act]" is.
  2. Use a concrete practice: wait one hour, walk outside, write before sending, sleep on it, or consult the day card first.
  3. Write when you will use it and what heat situation it interrupts.

## 29. The Wandering

- Key: the-wandering
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: I found, I found.
- Overview: A 30-day evening grief accompaniment: name the loss, search for what remains, and gently notice which capacities begin to open again.
- Confidence: The Wandering draws on Aset and Nebet-Het’s search, lament, and finding in the Pyramid Texts. This household flow accompanies grief without treating it as something to win.

- Glyph: 𓂻
- Routing: Best for grief, loss, mourning, missing, memory, breakup, searching, finding, restoration, support, body, and evening practice. Never a substitute for crisis support.
- Notes prefix: wandering
- Behavior kind: maat_wandering_event
- Burden: Very low
- Rhythm: Decan
- Special requirement: Grief accompaniment
- Safety note: If grief feels acute or unsafe, reach out to someone you trust, 988 in the US, or findahelpline.com internationally before returning here.
- Graph node slugs:
  1. maat
  2. aset
  3. nebet_het
  4. anpu
### 1. Name What Was Lost

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: The Search
- Title: Name What Was Lost
- Timing: Day 1 · evening
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The loss named specifically is the loss that can be searched for. Aset did not search for "what was gone" — she searched for Ausar. The name begins the search.
- Words: My brother, for I have searched for you.
- Steps:
  1. Write the name of what was lost. If it is not a person, name it as specifically as one.
  2. Write one sentence about what it gave you that you cannot get elsewhere right now.
  3. Place water nearby and drink it slowly after writing the name. The provision the body needs when it is doing this work.
- Source note: Aset's search began with Ausar's specific identity — not with the category of loss but with the specific person. The specific name makes the search possible.
- Requires real-world action: false

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"My brother, for I have searched for you."

Steps
1. Write the name of what was lost. If it is not a person, name it as specifically as one.
2. Write one sentence about what it gave you that you cannot get elsewhere right now.
3. Place water nearby and drink it slowly after writing the name. The provision the body needs when it is doing this work.
```

  3. Do not try to reopen it yet.

### 9. Stand Up

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Opening
- Title: Stand Up
- Timing: Day 29 · evening
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The Pyramid Texts command to stand is not a demand to stop grieving — it is the voice of love that has searched, found, and now asks the found one to rise. The physical act is the event.
- Words: Before standing: Stand up for me. Then stand. Then choose one act.
- Steps:
  1. Stand physically before logging.
  2. Choose one small act using a restored capacity: eat something wanted, listen to music, see something beautiful, or speak the name of what was lost to someone safe.
  3. Record the act.
- Source note: The command to stand in the Pyramid Texts is spoken by those who love the one who is down — Isis's command, Nephthys's command, Horus's command. The standing is invited by love that has found what it was looking for. This sitting receives that invitation and enacts it in one small act of the restored capacity.
- Requires real-world action: true

## 30. The Khat

- Key: the-khat
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: Teti is sound because of his body.
- Overview: A 30-day body-care practice: listen to the body, provide food, water, washing, rest, and movement, then close with a grounded body record.
- Confidence: The Khat draws on the physical restoration language of the Pyramid Texts: the body assembled, washed, fed, anointed, watered, and raised.

- Glyph: 𓀾
- Routing: Best for body care, tiredness, fatigue, sleep, water, food, movement, pain, tension, physical rest, exercise, and embodiment. Does not diagnose medical issues.
- Notes prefix: khat
- Behavior kind: maat_khat_event
- Burden: Low-Medium
- Rhythm: Decan
- Special requirement: Body-care practice
- Safety note: If your body is signaling something that may require medical attention, let this step support seeking care.
- Graph node slugs:
  1. maat
  2. ka
  3. hapy
  4. anpu
### 1. Listen to the Body

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Attend
- Title: Listen to the Body
- Timing: Day 1 · morning
- Duration: 8-8 min
- Purpose: The body is reporting something right now — not what you think it should be feeling, but what it is actually communicating in this moment.
- Words: Teti is sound because of his body.
- Steps:
  1. Sit or lie down. Move attention from feet upward to face. The inventory is not an assessment — it is the act of listening to a report the body has been trying to give.
  2. Move attention from feet to face.
  3. Write three things the body is communicating right now, without judging or correcting them.
- Source note: "Teti is sound because of his body." The soundness was located in the physical form — bones assembled, limbs collected, earth on the flesh cleared away. The khat is not the vessel for the other four parts; it is one of the five. This sitting attends to it as such.
- Requires real-world action: false

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Teti is sound because of his body."

Steps
1. Sit or lie down. Move attention from feet upward to face. The inventory is not an assessment — it is the act of listening to a report the body has been trying to give.
2. Move attention from feet to face.
3. Write three things the body is communicating right now, without judging or correcting them.
```

### 5. The Anointing

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: Provide
- Title: The Anointing
- Timing: Day 15 · morning
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The anointing was not comfort — it was the mechanism of bodily agency: "you shall make him have control of his body." The body that receives deliberate care is the body the person can actually inhabit.
- Words: Ointment returns attention to the body.
- Steps:
  1. After washing, apply oil, lotion, cream, or water to some part of the body with deliberate attention — not as grooming but as care. The Pyramid Texts anointed the forehead first because the forehead is the face the person presents to the world.
  2. Record what the act returned to your relationship with the body.
  3. Keep body details private by default.
- Source note: The Pyramid Texts formula for anointing is specific: "you shall make it pleasant for him, wearing you; you shall make him have control of his body." The oil was the mechanism of control — the body that receives deliberate care is available to the person's intention.
- Requires real-world action: true

- Event number: 7

- Timing: Day 21 · morning
- Duration: 20-20 min
- Purpose: The Pyramid Texts repeated this command because the rising is the primary physical act of restoration. Not the most dramatic, not the most strenuous — the most fundamental. Move before logging.
- Words: Speak this before beginning to move: Stand up, repel your earth, clear away your dust, raise yourself. Then begin.
- Steps:
  1. Move deliberately for at least 20 minutes before logging.
  2. Walking, stretching, dancing, swimming, lifting, gardening, or physical cleaning all count if sustained and attended to.
  3. Record what the movement returned to the body.
- Source note: The command to stand appears throughout the Pyramid Texts corpus with the same urgency — it is the foundational physical act that all others depend on. The twenty-minute movement demonstrates that the body can still do what the rite commands.

## 31. The Oracle

- Key: the-oracle
- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Tagline: Thutmose placed himself in the shadow of the Great God and rested. The god came to him in the night. He awoke and acted.
- Overview: A 30-day dream-question practice: prepare one specific oracle question, receive and record the night without early interpretation, then act on one grounded indication.
- Confidence: The Oracle draws on Kemetic dream incubation, dream books, and the Dream Stela of Thutmose IV.

- Glyph: 𓄔
- Routing: Best for dreams, sleep, oracle questions, guidance, signs, symbols, night records, waking impressions, uncertainty, and grounded action after interpretation.
- Notes prefix: oracle
- Behavior kind: maat_oracle_event
- Burden: Low
- Rhythm: Decan
- Special requirement: Dream question practice
- Safety note: If disturbing dream content appears, bring it to a qualified professional or trusted support before continuing.
- Graph node slugs:
  1. maat
  2. djehuty
  3. ba
  4. heka
### 1. Prepare the Reception Chamber

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Preparation
- Title: Prepare the Reception Chamber
- Timing: Day 1 · evening
- Duration: 10-10 min
- Purpose: Thutmose did not simply fall asleep near the Sphinx — he placed himself in the shadow deliberately. The preparation creates the conditions for reception.
- Words: I place myself in the shadow of the Great God.
- Steps:
  1. Before sleep, clear the space near your head.
  2. Place one small object there.
  3. Write your oracle question on paper and set it under or beside the object.
- Source note: The Dream Stela records that Thutmose rested in the shadow of the Great God — a deliberate spatial act before the dream. The incubation at Kemetic temples followed the same logic: you position yourself within the field of divine presence and wait. The reception chamber is that positioning, made domestic.
- Requires real-world action: true

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I place myself in the shadow of the Great God."

Steps
1. Before sleep, clear the space near your head.
2. Place one small object there.
3. Write your oracle question on paper and set it under or beside the object.

Complete
Mark Oracle 1 observed after the action in the steps is actually done.
```

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Preparation
- Title: The Pre-Sleep Purification
- Timing: Day 5 · evening
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: Purify before sleep and hold the question clearly.
- Words: The question is held clearly before sleep.
- Steps:
  1. Before sleep, wash your face and hands with cool water.
  2. Hold the oracle question clearly.
  3. Lie down with the question present.
- Requires real-world action: true

### 3. The Invocation

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Preparation
- Title: The Invocation
- Timing: Day 9 · evening
- Duration: 5-8 min
- Purpose: The oracle consultation was formal — a specific address to a specific presence, with a specific question, in a specific posture of receiving. This invocation follows that form.
- Words: The question is spoken once, then silence receives.
- Steps:
  1. Speak the invocation before lying down. Address who you are asking by name. Speak the question once, clearly, completely. Do not speak again before sleep.
  2. Address the deity, principle, Ba, Ka, Ma’at, or divine presence you are asking.
  3. Speak the question clearly once. Do not speak again before sleep.
- Source note: Temple dream incubation was a formal consultation — not a vague hope for inspiration but a specific request made in a specific place with specific preparation. The invocation formalizes the request so that what the night sends can be understood as responsive to it.
- Requires real-world action: true

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Address the deity, principle, Ba, Ka, Ma’at, or divine presence you are asking.
3. Speak the question clearly once. Do not speak again before sleep.

Complete
Mark Oracle 3 observed after the action in the steps is actually done.
```

### 4. Record Upon Waking

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Reception
- Title: Record Upon Waking
- Timing: Day 11 · morning
- Duration: 10-10 min
- Purpose: The dream disperses immediately. Record before anything else — before the phone, before speaking, before the day's logic takes over.
- Words: The night record is written before interpretation.
- Steps:
  1. Immediately upon waking — before the phone, before speaking to anyone, before anything — reach for the notebook and write. What images. What words. What feelings. What atmosphere. Do not interpret yet.
  2. Record images, words, feelings, colors, sequence, and atmosphere.
  3. Do not interpret yet.
- Source note: The temple dream-reader received the dreamer's account immediately upon waking — before the dream had been edited by the waking mind. The dream written in the first thirty seconds is more accurate than the dream reconstructed an hour later.
- Requires real-world action: true

#### Canonical Detail Text


- Timing: Day 15 · any time · 11:00 reminder

- Words: Not every dream is the oracle.
- Steps:
  1. Review the dream records.
  2. Mark possible oracle material: unusually vivid, direct address, clear relation to the question, or strong waking impression.
  3. Mark ordinary dreams honestly.

### 7. The Kemetic Framework

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Interpretation
- Title: The Kemetic Framework
- Timing: Day 21 · any time · 11:00 reminder
- Duration: 10-10 min
- Purpose: Interpret the recurring element through four grounded questions.
- Words: Interpretation serves action.
- Steps:
  1. Interpret the recurring element with four questions.
  2. Ask what it does, what principle it carries, how it relates to the oracle question, and what action it indicates.
  3. Do not treat disturbing dream content as definitive truth.
- Requires real-world action: false

### 9. Act on What Was Received

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Interpretation
- Title: Act on What Was Received
- Timing: Day 29 · any time · 11:00 reminder
- Duration: 10-15 min
- Purpose: The oracle completes when the action is taken. Thutmose cleared the sand from around the Sphinx. The dream that produces no action was not a consultation — it was a night's sleep.
- Words: He awoke and acted.
- Steps:
  1. Take the specific action indicated before logging this event.
  2. Write the complete oracle record: question, what the night sent, what it indicated, and what action was taken.
  3. If the oracle stayed unclear, record that honestly and do only the grounded action you can justify.
- Source note: Thutmose's stela records the dream and then records that he commanded the sand to be cleared. The action is what closes the Stela's account — not the dream, not the interpretation, but the act taken in response.
- Requires real-world action: true
- Completion labels: {oracle_complete: Oracle complete}
